    “To avoid large and unsustainable budget deficits, the nation will
    ultimately have to choose among higher taxes, modifications to
    entitlement programs such as Social Security and Medicare, less
    spending on everything else from education to defense, or some
    combination of the above. These choices are difficult, and it
    always seems easier to put them off—until the day they cannot be
    put off any more.”
                                           Ben Bernanke (April 2010)1



This warning from Federal Reserve Chairman Ben Bernanke gets it
half right—but also half wrong.
     Mr. Bernanke is right that if the profligate policies of Congress
and the Obama administration continue in their current direction, we
as a country will be caught between a proverbial rock and some hard
choices. But where his advice goes off course is to offer as the only
choice harsh budget and program cuts and painfully higher taxes.
There is a better way—and it is embodied in the basic message of this
book.
     As we have explained, the best way to restore America’s prosperty
is to ensure a robust rate of long-term economic growth commensu-
rate with our full potential GDP growth rate. The way to achieve this



                                   229


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


is to undertake the set of broad-based structural reforms that we have
identified in our Seeds of Prosperity policy blueprint.
    In identifying these reforms, we have used our GDP Growth
Drivers equation and Ten Levers of Growth to illustrate how a wide
range of fiscal, monetary, tax, trade, health care, and regulatory poli-
cies have led to the large structural imbalances that are keeping our
economy from realizing its potential. These structural imbalances,
with which you should now be very familiar, include overconsump-
tion, underinvestment, excess government spending, and chronic
trade imbalances.
     As we have also pointed out, these structural imbalances have
caused our economy to grow at a rate below its historical average over
the past decade. This growth deficit has led to a loss of almost ten mil-
lion jobs that could have been created. Moreover during this time, the
growth in real average median household income has failed to keep
up with the pace of previous decades.
    Our Seeds of Prosperity policy blueprint seeks to reverse this eco-
nomic stagnation, thereby avoiding the “rock and hard choices” trap.
It does so through a suite of policies designed to rebalance our econ-
omy and put us on the path toward long-term prosperity.
     This concluding chapter summarizes our blueprint. Our hope is
that you, dear reader, will carry this message to your elected represen-
tatives. In so doing, you can help press the case for a “middle ground”
policy strategy that will give us the best chance of political consensus
and economic success.


      Our Seeds of Destruction Problem
Chronic structural imbalances in the U.S. economy have led to a
decade of economic growth significantly below our potential GDP


                                  230


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
        HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


growth rate, stagnant wages for many, and unacceptably high levels of
unemployment.


        Our Seeds of Prosperity Solution
To reach our full potential GDP growth rate in a sustainable fashion
over the long term, we must rebalance our economy according to the
principles of our Ten Levers of Growth and the insights gained from
our analysis of the U.S. GDP Growth Drivers equation. Such a rebal-
ancing will require a set of reforms in the areas of monetary and fis-
cal policy, tax and trade policy, energy policy, entitlements, health
care, and financial regulation.

                  Recasting Monetary Policy
The Federal Reserve’s easy monetary policies over the past decade
have played a role and fueling a housing bubble in America’s struc-
tural imbalance of overconsumption.
    The Fed must turn its emphasis away from discretionary fine-tun-
ing and firmly embrace a policy that has as its goals price stability and
sustainable long-term growth.
    The Fed must also fiercely guard its independence from both the
White House and Capitol Hill and not succumb to the “political cycle”
of easy money creation.

         Rethinking the Use of a Fiscal Stimulus
We must wean ourselves from the idea that we can stimulate our way
to prosperity. Instead, we must embrace the principle that only
through structural reforms will we return to the path of long-term
prosperity.


                                  231


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


    To avoid the debilitating effects of the structural imbalance of
excessive government spending, we must shy away from further use of
discretionary fiscal stimulus while having the patience to allow auto-
matic stabilizers to do their job.
    If a further fiscal stimulus is deemed to be absolutely necessary, it
should strictly meet the test of being temporary, timely, and targeted.
    Over the longer term, in implementing any stimulus, we should
favor tax cuts to stimulate business investment over increased govern-
ment expenditures. This way is the best one to stimulate job creation
over both the short and longer term.
    We must make our government more effective while limiting
both its size and the size of our budget deficits and national debt, as
well as Americans’ tax burden. At present, we are going in exactly the
opposite direction of all these goals.

                 Unburdening Our Tax Policy
America’s complex income-based tax system reinforces all four major
structural imbalances in the U.S. economy. It discourages both saving
and investment while handcuffing American exporters. This limitation
occurs even as Washington’s enormous power to tax promotes a cult
of fiscal irresponsibility within the myopic confines of the Beltway.
However, the American economy will never return to full prosperity
until it completes a very broad-based tax reform.
    The failure to engage in broad-based tax reform reflects a failure
of both Democrats and Republicans to escape ideological strait-
jackets. Many Democrats have fallen in love with taxing both “the
rich” and “big business,” oblivious to the growth-stalling effects of
onerous taxes on savings, investment, entrepreneurship, and job cre-
ation. Many Republicans advocate cutting taxes in the current broken
system rather than acknowledge the need for a certain level of govern-
ment revenues and bold reforms.

                                  232


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
         HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


     Any broad-based tax reform should seek to broaden the tax base,
reduce marginal tax rates, and, most important from a growth-stimu-
lating perspective, remove any tax incentives against savings, invest-
ment, entrepreneurial risk-taking, and exports.
    One approach examined by President George W. Bush’s Advisory
Panel integrates a consumption tax component into our current
income tax structure. This “blended” approach cuts in half the num-
ber of tax brackets, reduces the highest marginal tax rate, and reduces
the tax rate on dividends, capital gains, and interest. For businesses, it
also allows expensing for all new investment and removes the
deductibility for interest payments, while providing for border tax
adjustments.
    Although this blended approach does not eliminate the double
taxation of capital income or the biases against savings and invest-
ment, it would significantly increase savings and capital formation and
the economic and wage growth that come with it.
    An even more effective approach that we favor extends the
blended approach to reward saving and capital investment more com-
pletely. For individuals, income would continue to be taxed with a
progressive rate structure. However, all forms of capital income
earned by individuals—interest, dividends, and capital gains—would
be exempt. For businesses, corporate profits would likewise continue
to be taxed. However, all capital investment would be fully expensed.
    From an economic point of view, such a “progressive consump-
tion tax” meets the most important challenge of stimulating growth by
providing maximum incentives for individual saving and business
investment while removing the double taxation of capital income.
    From a political point of view, this approach represents a “middle
ground” reform. Democrats can support it because it maintains the
progressivity of the current income tax structure. Republicans can



                                   233


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
                           SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


likewise support it because it is fiscally neutral and represents a far
more efficient form of taxation.

       Freeing Our Trade Policy from Mercantilist
                and Protectionist Chains
Given that America’s chronic trade deficits act as a brake on growth
and raise difficult issues related to a loss of America’s political sover-
eignty, it is critical that they be reduced.
     If we want to reduce America’s large and chronic trade deficits,
there are only two major ways to do so—by reducing America’s heavy
oil import dependence, and by shrinking America’s trade imbalance,
particularly with China.
    China engages in a wide range of protectionist and mercantilist
practices that make it very difficult for American businesses to com-
pete on a level, free-trade playing field and equally difficult for the
United States to reduce its trade deficit with China. For these rea-
sons, America’s best chance of reducing its non-oil-related trade
deficit lies with constructive trade reform with China.
     Because free trade is one of the most important of the Ten Levers
of Growth, both the president and Congress must emphasize that free
trade means full compliance with WTO rules. By compliance, we
mean not relying on any illegal export subsidies or currency manipu-
lation and ensuring the presence of strong protection of intellectual
property, strict prohibitions against forced technology transfer, rea-
sonable environmental and health and safety standards that meet
international norms, free and open access to each country’s domestic
markets, and an unrestricted global market in raw materials.
    A key reason China has been able to run a large trade surplus with
the United States is America’s extremely low saving rate. Boosting the
American saving rate should be part of our national economic overall
trade policy.

                                   234


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
        HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


              The Soft, Hard, and Smart Paths
              to Greater Energy Independence
America has 4.5% of the world’s population and possesses less than
3% of proven world oil reserves. But we consume almost 25% of
annual world oil production and depend on foreign imports for 65%
of our oil needs.
    This “oil addiction” reduces our GDP growth rate by helping stall
three of the main drivers of our GDP equation—consumption, invest-
ment, and net exports. If America truly wants to return to its full
potential GDP growth rate, we must significantly reduce our oil
import dependence.
    Complete energy independence (or “autarky”) is neither feasible
nor desirable. However, reducing our dependence on foreign oil to a
level more consistent with economic prosperity and national security
clearly is. We believe there is at least one simple but highly effective
market-driven, middle-ground solution that both Democrats and
Republicans can embrace.
    Many Democrats prefer to solve our energy problems using “soft-
path” options ranging from energy conservation and regulatory fixes
such as higher gas mileage standards to a greater reliance on renew-
ables such as solar and wind. In contrast, many Republicans prefer
“hard-path” options ranging from drilling for more oil and developing
more domestic natural gas to the increased use of coal and nuclear
power.
     We believe that the truly “smart path” to reducing our oil inde-
pendence lies in embracing both hard and soft path options. Our
“Law of Oil Import Substitution” states that as the price of imported
oil rises, America’s import dependence falls as both soft and hard path
oil substitutes become more economically attractive. This law leads us
to our market-driven policy solution:


                                  235


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                           SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


    To bring more domestic soft and hard path oil substitutes into the
market, we propose an oil price floor. To set this floor, the White
House, in conjunction with the U.S. Congress and the Department of
Energy, first determines the desired target rate of oil use and import
reduction. To achieve this targeted reduction, the target price floor is
set by imposing a flexible fee. This fee is equal to the difference
between the actual world price for a barrel of oil and the target price
necessary to achieve the desired reduction in oil use and import
dependence.
     Such a flexible fee is quite different from the kind of permanent
taxes on oil imports (and gasoline) that have frequently been pro-
posed. It is levied only when the world price of oil falls below the tar-
get level. As the world price of oil moves toward the target price, the
fee is reduced. Moreover, whenever the world price of oil rises above
the target level, the fee is removed, and the desired level of oil use and
import reduction is achieved purely through market forces.
    This market-based flexibility makes our proposed oil price floor a
far more politically palatable solution than any permanent tax on
either oil imports or gasoline. Neither of these has broad support
among the general public.
    We also believe that any revenues raised from such a fee should
be returned to the American people in the form of tax cuts—thereby
making the foreign oil tax revenue-neutral.
     Democrats can embrace such an approach because it fulfills the
goals of promoting both energy conservation and environmental
stewardship and the development of alternative energy sources. Its
flexible nature likewise reduces concerns about the regressivity of
such a fee and any transient negative impacts on the poor.
    Republicans can embrace such a proposal because it is market-
driven, revenue-neutral, and applied only when the price of oil rises
above the target. It also removes the need for any elaborate regulatory

                                   236


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
         HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


programs such as fuel efficiency standards for cars. Furthermore, it
removes the need to heavily subsidize any of a number of import
substitutes—whether corn-based ethanol, coal-to-liquid synfuels, or
renewables.

                 (Truly) Saving Social Security
America’s three largest entitlement programs—Social Security,
Medicare, and Medicaid—face looming funding deficits that will
threaten their very existence and the nation’s fiscal health over time.
Despite this looming crisis, our political leaders continue to expand
America’s entitlement programs rather than rein them in—for exam-
ple, the Bush Medicare drug bill in 2003 and ObamaCare in 2010.
     By our Iron Law of Entitlement Reform, any attempt to close the
looming entitlement spending gap by further raising taxes, running
large budget deficits, or cutting spending on critical needs such as
education and infrastructure will backfire. The result will be less inno-
vation and entrepreneurship, slower economic growth, lower wages,
fewer people employed, less tax revenue collected, and an even larger
budget deficit.
    Two policy options, working in combination, have the potential to
close all, or almost all, of the forecast spending gap for Social Security.
     First, we should raise the retirement age to better match rising
life expectancies—but we should do so in a gradual and orderly fash-
ion, as a matter of equity. The argument for this is self-evident. When
Social Security was established in 1935, the retirement age was set at
65—roughly the same as the life expectancy of the average American.
Today, average life expectancy has risen to close to 80, yet the retire-
ment age has been raised to only 67.
    Second, we should index initial Social Security benefits to prices
rather than wages so as to maintain real, inflation-adjusted purchasing


                                   237


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


power for beneficiaries. Because wages have historically risen signifi-
cantly faster than prices, the current method of wage indexing
amounts to an extra boost in purchasing power for retirees. However,
this boost has not been properly funded by the payroll taxes that
retirees and their employers have paid into the system.
    Price indexing is a fair way to address this problem because it
does not result in any loss in real purchasing power by beneficiaries.
Instead, by the very definition of price indexing, benefits keep pace
with the rate of inflation. In this way, the real purchasing power and
standard of living of retirees are preserved.
    This combination of raising the retirement age and moving to a
price-indexing system would close most or even all of the Social
Security spending gap. And we can use some of the savings to
strengthen benefits for low-income individuals. This combination
would do so with the least damage to our economy and in a way that
would guarantee greater intergenerational equity and thereby reduce
inaction due to future political conflict.

                 More Focus and Flexibility
                 for Medicare and Medicaid
The most important way to stop Medicare and Medicaid from devour-
ing an ever-larger share of America’s economic pie is to bring health
care costs under control. We can also make at least some progress by
targeting Medicare and Medicaid spending more at lower-income
households.
     For Medicaid, flexibility is the key. A more flexible approach
means moving to a block-grant-type structure in which the federal
government annually gives each state the Medicaid funding it
received the year before, adjusted for inflation and for changes in the
state’s population of low-income individuals—with few or no strings
attached.

                                  238


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
         HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


    For Medicare, the watchwords are cost-consciousness and focus.
Cost-consciousness means setting an explicit annual budget for
Medicare. This alternative is preferable to our current “mandatory”
spending frame of mind, which is simply inconsistent with the pro-
ductivity enhancements and cost control we need.
    To focus Medicare better, we must also move away from the cur-
rent fee-for-service reimbursement system. Instead, Medicare could
give each retiree support for a basic health plan, which individuals could
supplement at their own expense. Support should focus on lower-
income individuals, with much less assistance for more affluent seniors.

                Starting Over on Health Care
Even though America spends almost twice as much per person on
health care as many other major industrial countries, the quality of
our health care lags behind most other countries. We can measure this
by yardsticks as disparate as infant mortality, survival rates for major
diseases, and deaths due to medical error.
    President Barack Obama was right to pursue health care reform.
America’s costly health care system doesn’t deliver enough value for
the money spent. However, the legislation he slipped through
Congress is just plain wrong.
    The primary thrust of ObamaCare is to expand health insurance
coverage rather than containing costs. This new and massive entitle-
ment will be paid for in the two worst possible ways—by further
increasing the budget deficit and by further raising taxes. The
inevitable consequences will be to exacerbate our health care cost
problem while stalling the economy.
    Because health care costs are rising so fast, politicians inevitably
turn to raising taxes to finance programs such as Medicare and
Medicaid. For example, under ObamaCare, the Medicare payroll tax


                                   239


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


will jump from 1.45% to 2.35% for upper-income filers. ObamaCare
will also slap a 3.8% tax onto capital gains, interest income, and divi-
dends starting in 2013. These tax hikes will depress and thereby slow
down the rate of innovation, job creation, and wage growth.
     There is a clear market-driven path toward reducing health care
costs while improving medical care quality. Moving down this path
will require individuals to take much greater responsibility for their
health care. Also, health insurers and health care providers must face
more fully the competitive forces in the marketplace.
    The three market-driven policy changes described next go a long
way toward achieving the objectives of lower-cost and higher-quality
care.

Eliminate the Tax Code Bias for Employer-Provided Insurance
Under today’s employer-provided insurance system, neither employ-
ers nor employees pay income or payroll taxes on the employer’s con-
tribution to an employee’s insurance plan. These tax preferences
create large incentives for employees to purchase much more expen-
sive employer-provided insurance plans with low deductibles and low
copayment rates rather than “true insurance” plans geared primarily
toward covering catastrophic health events.
     Within this system, $5 out of every $6 of health care spending is
paid for by someone other than the person receiving care—insurance
companies, employers, or the government. The resultant price insen-
sitivity of consumers leads to a whole range of wasteful medical
practices and the overconsumption of low-value-added health care
services.
    Eliminating one of America’s most enduring and lucrative tax
preferences is a huge political hot potato. Given this high hurdle, we
propose a more palatable “second-best” way to address the clear bias


                                  240


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
        HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


toward employer-provided health plans. All health spending could be
tax-deductible—including out-of-pocket payments, purchases of indi-
vidual insurance, and purchases of COBRA coverage.
     Such deductibility mitigates the tax bias against individual insur-
ance, because both employer-sponsored and individual insurance can
be acquired with pre-income tax dollars. The tax change also
enhances the fairness of the federal income tax system. Ultimately,
allowing health care expenses to be tax-deductible should help reduce
the growth in health costs, because greater cost consciousness leads to
less spending.

Remove State Barriers
“Mandated benefits laws” in some states require health plans to cover
particular types of persons, services, or providers such as chiroprac-
tors. But such mandates increase health insurance costs by at least
5%, and possibly as much as 15%, by muting market signals. They
should be repealed.
    “Any willing provider” laws in some states require health plans to
accept bills from any doctor, hospital, or pharmacist who is willing to
accept the plan’s terms and conditions. Studies indicate such laws like-
wise increase costs by 1% to 2% by weakening the cost-containment
effects of managed-care plans. Likewise, they should be repealed.
    State-by-state regulation also raises costs by reducing competition
among insurance companies and by allowing state legislatures to
impose insurance mandates that raise prices while preventing resi-
dents from getting policies more suitable for their needs. Individuals
should, however, be allowed to buy health insurance offered in states
other than those in which they live or in a national market. This
change would increase competition and lower insurance prices.




                                  241


                                            Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


Reform Malpractice Laws
Our system of excessive litigation leads to higher costs and fewer
choices for patients because of “defensive medicine.” Ironically, it also
leads to more, rather than fewer, medical errors by systematically sup-
pressing the collection and analysis of precisely the kind of hospital
and patient data that would otherwise help reduce medical errors.
    We propose a reasonable national cap on noneconomic damages
in medical malpractice lawsuits. This would go a long way toward
reducing costs and decreasing unnecessary defensive medicine.
     We also propose limiting the discoverability of data in legal pro-
ceedings that have been collected for the purposes of improving qual-
ity and reducing medical errors. Such legal protection will encourage
health care organizations to develop policies to collect and analyze
information about medical errors. It also will encourage doctors,
nurses, and other health care workers to report mistakes. Passing such
legislation would be an important step toward reducing medical
errors.
    Likewise, we believe that both patients and health care providers
should be given more freedom to experiment with alternatives to liti-
gation such as alternative dispute resolution. This form of arbitration
would replace the current “compensation lottery” with a more consis-
tent decision-making process.

                            *      *      *
    The three types of health care reform policies we propose offer
considerable advantages over ObamaCare. Instead of raising health
care costs, they fundamentally change incentives among individuals,
insurers, and providers to gradually slow the growth in costs by reduc-
ing inefficient demand without sacrificing quality and innovation.
Instead of radically changing health care overnight, they take an

incremental approach, respecting the tremendous uncertainty sur-
rounding the effectiveness of different approaches to rein in costs.

           Preventing Another Housing Bubble
                   and Financial Crisis
It was not “too little regulation” that caused the financial crisis.
Instead, the lack of effective regulation in the face of a set of over-
whelmingly powerful perverse incentives led astray many actors in the
housing bubble drama. Failing to understand this—and failing to
understand that the primary goal of financial regulation should be to
reduce the likelihood of crises—has resulted in yet another massive
piece of legislation by the Obama administration that widely misses
the mark.
    As with ObamaCare, this legislation represents a significant
expansion of the government bureaucracy. It establishes no fewer
than five new government agencies and entities even as it fails to
reduce the chance of “too big to fail” bailouts. Following an Occam’s
Razor approach, we offer a much simpler and more direct route to
reform based on an analysis of the causes of the 2007–2009 crisis.

#1: Better Monetary Policy
The U.S. housing bubble and its recessionary aftermath were made
worse by easy money policies of the U.S. Federal Reserve. One of the
best ways to avoid a reoccurrence is for the Fed to adopt a less accom-
modative and more monetary policy in economic recoveries and be
more cognizant of the affects of its actions on financial stability.

#2: More Money Down
Because many homeowners had little or no equity in their homes, it
was far too easy for them to simply walk away when the bubble burst.


                                  243


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


To avoid this problem of “no skin in the game,” we should adopt a
variation of the successful Canadian model: Homeowners should be
required to put down at least 20% as a down payment—or purchase
mortgage loan insurance.

#3: An End to Exotic Mortgages
The proliferation of a wide range of exotic subprime mortgage lend-
ing instruments—from adjustable-rate mortgages and balloon mort-
gages to interest-only loans and “liar loans”—allowed people who
could not afford to buy a house to do just that. We do not need to cre-
ate a huge Consumer Protection bureaucracy to solve this problem, as
the new financial reform law has done. Rather, we need to prohibit or
restrict high-risk mortgage products and lending practices.

#4: More Transparency for Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac
In the 2007–2009 crisis, flawed mathematical models grossly underes-
timated risk and worked in conjunction with a host of new and equally
exotic financial instruments such as mortgage-backed securities
(MBSs). This led to bailouts of government-sponsored entities such as
Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac. One clear lesson is that it is inappro-
priate for these government-sponsored entities to allow their share-
holders to benefit from high risk-taking while taxpayers are forced to
bail them out. If we are to have a public presence in the housing mar-
ket, it should be transparent, and the firms should hedge their balance
sheet risk. However, the Obama administration and new financial
reform law offer little guidance here.

#5: Improving the Credit Rating Agencies
A second type of exotic instrument—the collateralized debt obligation
(CDO)—helped lead to the collapse of Wall Street icons such as Bear
Stearns and Lehman Brothers. Despite the high risk, CDO investors

                                  244


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
         HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


were told by supposedly reputable credit agencies such as Dun &
Bradstreet, Fitch, Moody’s Investor Service, and Standard & Poor’s
that their CDO securities were “investment grade.”
     We must ensure more accurate credit ratings and far greater dis-
closure of just exactly what information credit ratings are based on—
particularly for structured finance securities. CRAs therefore should
be required to make extensive disclosure of the criteria, methodolo-
gies, models, processes, key assumptions, and scenario analyses they
employ in rating all types of securities. Despite some positive features
of the new financial reform law in these areas, it encourages litigation
instead of urging the SEC to do its job.

#6: A Centralized Clearinghouse for Derivatives
Collateralized debt obligations played a key role in the collapse of the
housing bubble and the ensuing financial crisis (as well as the AIG
bailout). However, they would not have done nearly as much damage
without the existence of another exotic lending instrument—the
credit default swap (CDS). Because CDS derivatives are important
tools for both measuring and diversifying credit risk, they should not
be banned, as many critics have called for. Rather, the focus should be
on reducing systemic risk, which is the risk of collapse of an entire sys-
tem or entire market, exacerbated by links and interdependencies.
      The best way to do so is through a “centralized clearinghouse.”
Such a clearinghouse would enhance the liquidity and transparency of
the CDS market by actively managing daily collateral requirements of
clearinghouse members and the netting of positions between and
among them. Yes, the Treasury Department has pledged to subject all
standardized OTC derivative contracts—particularly CDSs. But thus
far, it has exercised little leadership in centralized clearing.




                                   245


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


#7: More Flexible Bank Capital Reserves
By providing a cushion against losses, capital reserves are supposed to
act as a first line of defense against bank failures and their knock-on
consequences for systemic risk. One of the most important flaws of
the current regulatory framework for setting minimum capital levels
is that reserve levels are fixed rather than flexible over the course of
the business cycle. This inflexibility forces banks to raise capital in a
business cycle downturn, and as losses mount, capital levels are
depleted. The result is that any contraction in the economy is further
amplified by an increasing inability of the banks to lend.
    We propose a shift to business-cycle-sensitive capital require-
ments to avoid a boom-bust cycle from inflexible capital limits. One
way to do this is to require financial institutions to build up reserves
during booms. A second alternative is to raise contingent capital dur-
ing a downturn by converting unsecured debt to equity. In addition,
because large or important institutions pose systemic risks, they
should be required to hold a larger capital buffer.

#8: Eliminating “Too Big to Fail”
As the failure of Lehman and the rescue of AIG indicated, we have
had significant trouble resolving the failure of big, complicated finan-
cial institutions. This brick in the wall of “too big to fail” must be
replaced. Otherwise, we will repeat the destructive process of massive
bailouts that we witnessed during the last crisis.
     The Obama Treasury has proposed the creation of an additional
insolvency regime with powers similar to those available for banks that
can be invoked when a financial company’s insolvency poses a systemic
risk to market. However, the Treasury’s proposals are inadequate. A
better alternative is the implementation of a comprehensive Financial
Company Resolution Act, applicable to all financial institutions.


                                  246


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
         HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


     The new financial reform law, while too big to read, offers little to
deal with the elephant in the room of too-big-to-fail. While the law
tackles resolution authority, it lacks key specifics. (At least the law jet-
tisoned the Obama administration’s call to capitalize a large “orderly
liquidation fund” in advance; this was an open invitation to bailouts.)

#9: Replacing a Fragmented and Sectoral Regulatory Model
Most other leading financial center countries have moved toward con-
solidated financial oversight. But the United States remains saddled
with a fragmented and sectoral model of regulation. This model was
woefully unprepared to deal with the 2007–2009 financial crisis and
all the problems of widespread systemic risk it entails. The clear les-
son from the crisis—and the exceedingly slow reaction times of our
key but fragmented regulatory institutions—is that effective financial
regulation going forward will inevitably require a reorganization of the
current regulatory structure.
    The new financial reform law vests systemic risk regulation in an
interagency council composed of several existing regulatory agencies,
but this approach is likely to fail in preventing crises. This important
role should be retained by the Federal Reserve for the simple but
powerful reason that one regulator, and one regulator alone, needs
both the authority and accountability to regulate matters pertaining to
systemic risk.
      We also propose a new U.S. Financial Services Authority
(USFSA) that would regulate all aspects of the financial system,
including market structure and activities and safety and soundness for
all financial institutions. (It also might possibly regulate consumer and
investor protection with respect to financial products if this responsi-
bility were lodged with the USFSA.)
   The new law’s placement of a Consumer Protection Agency in the
Fed is cynical. Its primary purpose is to obtain off-balance-sheet Fed

                                   247


                                               Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


funding from the profits the Fed makes from monetary policy and
other services (over $50 billion in 2009). Earmarking Fed profits for
particular purposes sets a bad precedent from the standpoint of
accountability, as does a system in which the Fed’s name is on policies
it doesn’t control.

#10: Curtailing Subsidies for Nonproductive Investment
One of the central themes of this book is that we need to fix chronic
structural imbalances in our GDP Growth Drivers equation.
Unfortunately, the current tax code encourages too much investment
in relatively nonproductive capital projects such as residential housing
through the mortgage interest rate deduction. It also discourages
investment in productive capital through high marginal corporate
income tax rates and high individual income tax rates on capital gains,
dividends, and interest income.
     Although we understand that homeownership is a sacred cow of
the American political fabric, we also believe that it is long past time
to revisit our policies on the mortgage interest rate deduction. Our
proposal is not to do away with the mortgage interest rate deduction.
Rather, it is to limit the deduction to a family’s primary residence and
disallow such deductions for second homes. And Congress and the
president should advance the cause of tax reform to reduce the costly
tax bias against business investment.


                          Conclusion
We wrote this book because we are deeply concerned about our coun-
try’s economic future. Our concern stems from the inability of so
many of our leaders to understand this simple truth:




                                  248


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
        HOW TO IMPLEMENT OUR SEEDS OF PROSPERITY POLICY BLUEPRINT


      Improvements in the American economy, like the economies of
all nations of the world, depend on growth in income. This growth
comes from increases in standards of living and is driven by four com-
ponents: consumption, business investment, government spending,
and net exports. Business investment and net exports are particularly
important in terms of providing the innovation, increased productiv-
ity, job creation, and long-term growth that we need to be a prosper-
ous nation. Because the Washington establishment fails to understand
this simple truth, it continues to adopt more and more onerous tax
and regulatory policies that suppress business investment while bloat-
ing our government, discouraging savings, and destroying the compet-
itive advantages we need to boost our export trade.
     Our leaders have often sidestepped important, difficult reforms.
Instead, we have been treated to policies that encourage consump-
tion—even if that consumption is unsustainable—instead of policies
that will raise all our incomes and secure our future. We deserve bet-
ter than this from our political elites. And our children and their chil-
dren also deserve much better.
    This book has described a better way that will shift us from the
current “Seeds of Destruction” agenda in which we are now engaged
onto the path of our Seeds of Prosperity blueprint.
     The message will be heard only if, after reading this book, you
also speak out—and urge our political leaders to understand what this
country needs to build a strong economy and act on it.
    If not now, when?

An Interview with Glenn Hubbard
         About His Time
       in the White House

Conducted June 1, 2010

NAVARRO: Hello, Glenn.
I want to talk with you today mostly about your experiences in the
White House, and I want to just start by exploring some of the cir-
cumstances as to how you were actually appointed as Chairman of the
Council of Economic Advisers. For example, when did you first meet
President George W. Bush? Did you help him as an economic advisor
on his campaign in 2000? Or did you come into contact with him some
other way?

HUBBARD: I worked with then-Governor Bush on his presidential
campaign really from the beginning. I met him in late 1998 before the
campaign and then worked with him throughout his campaign.

NAVARRO: And how does that go when an economist such as your-
self is working on a campaign? Were you actually out in Texas? Did
you travel with him at all, or was your role all done by e-mail and
phone and teleconferences?

HUBBARD: I went to Austin a good bit throughout 1999. Really,
there was a whole team of us who advised Governor Bush on a lot of
issues, and I also did some travel with him on the campaign trail.




                                269


                                          Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


NAVARRO: Who else was on that team?

HUBBARD: A lot of very good people. John Taylor, John Cogan,
Larry Lindsey, Marty Feldstein, Michael Boskin, Tim Muris, among
others.

NAVARRO: And they all came through Austin at one time or another?

HUBBARD: Yes.

NAVARRO: Now once you were the Chairman of the Council of
Economic Advisers, how often did you actually meet with President
Bush at the White House? Was it once a day, once a week, or some-
thing in between?

HUBBARD: Well, it obviously varied with the president’s schedule.
Basic economic briefings were at least once a week, and then there
were periodic meetings as economic issues arose. During the time I
was there, for good or for bad, economics was often on the top of the
list, so my contact with the president was pretty frequent.

NAVARRO: And when you went in to see President Bush, was it basi-
cally a one-on-one type of experience, or did you go in with a team, or
how did that work?

HUBBARD: It depended. With general economic briefings, there
would often be the whole economic team there. I might present the
briefing while others would offer their views. Other times, it would be
one-on-one, while sometimes we’d have the Chief of Staff in the room
as well. It just depended on what suited the president for the issue.

NAVARRO: Now I know Larry Lindsey was part of that team. Who
were the other major economic players on the team while you were
there?



                                  270


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


HUBBARD: Well, Paul O’Neill was Secretary of the Treasury when I
first started. And Mitch Daniels was the OMB director. Josh Bolten at
the time was the deputy chief of staff. He was also very involved in
economic policy conversations. Later on, John Snow became Treasury
Secretary, and Steve Friedman became Director of the National
Economic Council after Larry Lindsey left.

NAVARRO: And was that group a loose confederation, or did you
function as a team? If so, who was the head of the team, and how did
it all work?

HUBBARD: Well, the president is always the head of the team. So in
that sense his economic advisors are more of a confederation.
However, we did meet as a team, and we’d usually present the presi-
dent with a memo in advance with our views. If we disagreed, we’d
tell him who felt which way and why—and then, of course, the final
decision would be his.

NAVARRO: Historically, we know that different presidents have had
different levels of interest in economics. For example, both Bill
Clinton and John F. Kennedy were reported to be very astute on the
economy, while, ironically, FDR expressed fairly little interest, even
though the country was in the Great Depression. How would you
describe George W. Bush on the economy? Did he have a high level
of interest? Was he immediately able to grasp fairly complicated
issues?

HUBBARD: I’d say he was very interested in the economy.
Particularly in how economic issues affected real people. He was a nat-
ural-born politician. He was perhaps less interested in the finer points
of economics—you know, an argument over elasticities wouldn’t
exactly rivet the president—but the overall question of the economy
did.


                                  271


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


NAVARRO: Did he generally take your advice?

HUBBARD: I think it depends on the issue. I think on tax policy and
budget policy, yes. Health care, yes. There were certain issues he
didn’t. The steel tariffs that he put forth I certainly opposed. So he
didn’t always take my advice. And you don’t expect that. Economics
was just one element in his decision process, and advisors have to
understand that the final decision is up to the boss.

NAVARRO: And how about your relationship with Dick Cheney? I
saw at least one press account that indicated your conversations about
the economy with Cheney went into a lot more detail than those with
the president. Is that a fair characterization? And just how much
power did Cheney have on economic issues? Did he even sit in on
those economic meetings?

HUBBARD: Well, I’d say that Vice President Cheney was very inter-
ested in economics as well as the economy. He did enjoy those con-
versations. He’s somebody that I had known longer than President
Bush as well. I would describe him as very interested in economics
and very influential, as you would expect from any good vice presi-
dent. Yes, he often sat in on briefings with the president. He also got
to stay when we all left. So that’s significant agenda-setting power.

NAVARRO: And were there separate briefings for Cheney on the
same issues at times?

HUBBARD: Not nearly as regularly as for President Bush, but cer-
tainly, whenever the vice president wanted a briefing on any economic
subject, he got it. Periodically, he received general economic briefings
as well.

NAVARRO: Let’s turn to the topic of the tax cuts. As the chairman of
the Council of Advisers, you were generally recognized in the media


                                  272


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


as the primary mover behind the Bush tax cuts. And Fred Barnes also
called you the most influential chairman of the council in two
decades. How critical a role did you yourself play in designing and
helping to pass the Bush tax cuts?

HUBBARD: I think economists have to be modest here. The original
Bush tax cuts—the 2001 tax cuts—came during Governor Bush’s cam-
paign, and they certainly reflected the thinking of a number of peo-
ple. I previously gave you a list of what I think was an A-team to help
Governor Bush in the campaign [Taylor, Cogan, Feldstein, Boskin,
among others]. I focused a lot on marginal tax rate cuts, and then later
tax cuts on investment and dividends and capital gains tax cuts. But I
think really the credit has to go to the president. He’s the one who
took the political heat and got out in front of the issue. So, it’s really
all him.

NAVARRO: And during the presidential campaign before the reces-
sion hit, I think it’s fair to say that he campaigned for tax cuts as sup-
ply-side tax cuts. But it may also be fair to say that once President
Bush was in office and the economy was in trouble, he also marketed
them from a Keynesian demand-side point of view. Do you think that’s
a fair characterization or not?

HUBBARD: I think that’s a fair statement. It certainly reflected a cou-
ple of influences. One, there was some tension among his advisors as
to how to describe tax cuts. But I think he also saw a “stimulus”
description as very relevant to the problems of the day. The president
is not only a policy maker. He has to be someone who communicates
policy to the public.

NAVARRO: At the time, the tax cuts were criticized for the potential
of exacerbating the budget deficit. And there were ten Nobel
Laureate economists and over 400 other economists who signed a


                                  273


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


letter criticizing the tax cuts. Do you have any thoughts now on that
debate and that criticism from the perspective of history?

HUBBARD: I think it really depends on what your baseline is. I dis-
agreed with those Nobel Laureates, respectfully, because the right
baseline was not that any surplus would be saved, as a down payment
on entitlement reform and debt reduction. As a point of fact, the
budget surpluses would likely have been spent. And the question at
the time really was this: If you believed the [budget] surplus forecasts
of the time, would you rather lock in new spending or have tax cuts?
To me, that was a no-brainer. The only real question was what kind of
tax cuts we should have. Many of the tax cuts from the 2001 Act were
good policy. However, some of those cuts were less good policy,
although they may have been better politics.

NAVARRO: Which tax cuts were the ones that were less than good
policy?

HUBBARD: I am thinking of the new 10 percent bracket, which was
really just a lump-sum tax cut for most people; that’s not particularly
efficient in the end. And child tax credits were good politics, but
there’s not much economic lift there. It really depends on what you
want a tax cut to do. I had heard Governor Bush say, “I want tax pol-
icy to encourage growth.” To me that’s best accomplished through
marginal rate cuts on work, saving, investment, and entrepreneurship.
Those were the tax cuts I thought were much more exciting. And
those are why I really disagreed with the critics of the tax cuts both in
2001 and 2003.

NAVARRO: There was an interesting blog entry by Professor Brad
DeLong from UC-Berkeley on this issue of good versus bad policy. It
described an interaction between you and the president. You
expressed your opinion on a particular tax issue and described it as


                                  274


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


bad policy, and that provoked a rather strenuous response from
President Bush chastising you, at least according to DeLong. Was
there any truth to that episode, and what was behind it?

HUBBARD: I don’t really recall that precise episode. If the president
said something that I thought was bad policy, I told him so. It was not
because I wanted to offend him, but because I felt it was my job. At
the end of the day, he must make the decisions, and he did make the
decisions, but I thought he should have the advice of his experts. The
job of an economist is not to try and impose your own political judg-
ment on the president, the political leader. It’s to tell him what you
really think.

NAVARRO: So the idea of him chastising you for that kind of thing is
inaccurate at best?

HUBBARD: I never remember being chastised. It’s more a matter of
that I could easily remember being overruled by him.... But I think he
appreciated the candor of his advisors.

NAVARRO: One last question about the tax cuts. Why wasn’t the
AMT [alternative minimum tax] problem permanently fixed? From a
political point of view, because of the AMT, a lot of upper-level tax-
payers really didn’t feel a lot of relief, yet the media was able to get
into a frenzy about the package being nothing but tax handouts for the
rich. Any thoughts about that issue and why the AMT wasn’t
addressed?

HUBBARD: Well, I think part of it was that the original design of the
tax cuts was during a political campaign, not when the president was
actually in office, and so the rhetoric of the tax cuts was as important
as the substance of the tax cuts. The AMT was obviously a big prob-
lem, and the sunset provision was obviously a big problem. There



                                  275


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                           SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


were a number of practical problems with the tax cuts. But they were
designed in a campaign.

NAVARRO: Let’s turn to probably the most important topic that hap-
pened while you were at the White House—the 9/11 terrorist attacks.
Do you remember where you were the morning of September 11,
2001?

HUBBARD: I definitely do. It’s not something you can easily forget.
I was in my office, in the Old Executive Office Building, and I
remember seeing on the television a clip of the first plane hitting the
World Trade Center. My thought at the time was this was some sort
of an accident. And then I received some information from official
sources indicating a more sinister concern and commencing an evac-
uation from the White House. I remember it because one of my sen-
ior staff was on crutches, and I slowly had to help carry him down
three flights of stairs. I won’t forget that day easily.

NAVARRO: So you were actually evacuated offsite from the White
House?

HUBBARD: We were evacuated because, at the time, there was a
fear that a plane would be coming for the White House, and we only
had minutes to leave. So we gathered essential things and then left.
Some of us as advisors met at an agreed-upon site, reserved in
advance for crisis planning. The president, of course, wasn’t in
Washington that day.

NAVARRO: After that, how did the economic team operate? For
example, did you yourself participate in the decision to close down the
stock market? Were there any special considerations at the time? Give
us a little insight into how all that went, because it was one of the most
difficult times in our history.



                                   276


                                              Download from www.wowebook.com
    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


HUBBARD: Well, there were a number of considerations. You men-
tioned closing the stock exchanges. However, keeping in contact with
leaders of the Federal Reserve was probably one of the most con-
structive conversations. Vice Chairman Roger Ferguson led that con-
versation for the Fed; in the case of a panic, we had to make sure cash
was supplied, and make sure the Federal Reserve was cognizant of the
situation. There was a lot of debate over whether the stock exchanges
should be closed. I wrote an op-ed article on the day of the reopening
of the New York stock exchange, in which I argued the value of U.S.
equities really shouldn’t fall that much because the actual economic
impact of the crash wasn’t so significant. I don’t think the market lis-
tened, as it did drop a lot that day!

NAVARRO: How long did that crisis footing on the economy last?
Were you hunkered down for a week, two weeks, a month trying to
deal with the immediate aftermath of 9/11?

HUBBARD: It was actually for a very long time, because, from the
economic perspective—as opposed to the literal counterterrorism
perspective—the damage was really ongoing. Even on the day of 9/11,
I remember sitting down with two colleagues just thinking, “Okay, you
know, what are we going to do about the airline industry? What about
incurring terrorism risk? What’s our follow-on thinking about fiscal
policy?” That discussion really went on for quite a while. In early
2002, President Bush put forth a series of tax changes; there were
some interventions in the airline industry. All of that policy action
came out of 9/11.

NAVARRO: 9/11 was also the catalyst for the Iraq war. What was the
role of the Bush economic team in the debate over whether to invade
Iraq? Were the economic impacts of the war ever considered in the
decision to enter that war?



                                  277


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                            SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


HUBBARD: Certainly, the economic impacts of the war were one
question. The president asked, and we answered, as best we could.
However, I don’t think economics played the lead role in deciding to
invade Iraq. Nor should it. Economists are experts on the discussion
of economic impacts, but we [the economics team] really played very
little role there. I felt at the time, and still feel, that there was too lit-
tle attention paid to economic issues considering the reconstruction of
Iraq. There’s two ways to think of the economic impact of the war in
Iraq. One is the war’s impact on the U.S. economy. That was
answered. But also, how do you actually redesign an economic struc-
ture in Iraq? That question had too little pre-attack planning.

NAVARRO: Were you mindful of that lack of attention to the eco-
nomic reconstruction at that time?

HUBBARD: Very much. Obviously one understands that military
actions take precedent. But for me I never doubted the U.S. military
could subjugate Iraq. The economic question for me was recon-
struction.

NAVARRO: How were you able to express your views about economic
reconstruction during that time? I mean, were you sitting in the same
room with Donald Rumsfeld and Paul Wolfowitz and Dick Cheney
and all the strategists for that? Was the rest of the economic team
there for that? I mean, how did that work?

HUBBARD: It depends on the meetings. The president had many
more meetings without economists or domestic policy advisors.
However, there were meetings with national security and military offi-
cials and economic team members to at least give the president all
points of view.

NAVARRO: How did you become aware of the issue of economic
reconstruction? I mean, that was a very astute insight that you had at

                                     278


                                                Download from www.wowebook.com
    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


the time. But it would be hard to come to that insight without some
input from the discussions.

HUBBARD: Well, really, it was more of having a sense of history. I
was thinking back to the World War II experience and how vastly dif-
ferent the approach we took with the Marshall Plan in Europe was
versus our more hands-off approach in Japan following World War II.
And I felt that the idea of a more active presence in economic recon-
struction, encouraging private business and entrepreneurship, was
something that you didn’t want to think about after you got there.
You’d want to think about it in advance.

NAVARRO: Was Paul Bremmer around at that time at all?

HUBBARD: Bremmer came on afterwards. He may well have had a
lot of discussions with the president about reconstruction.

NAVARRO: So he wasn’t part of that initial discussion at all?

HUBBARD: Not that I can remember.

NAVARRO: And how about Richard Clarke? Do you remember him
at all during the whole thing? Because he was one of the prominent
nay votes on a lot of the issues.

HUBBARD: With due respect to Mr. Clarke, his reaction today may
not be the same as I recall him having at the time, but I’ll leave that
to him. His expertise is more on terrorism.

NAVARRO: As far as Donald Rumsfeld and Paul Wolfowitz, did they
ever seek your advice or input on the economic implications of the
war?

HUBBARD: Well, they certainly received the same information that
the president did. I don’t recall any separate requests from them.


                                  279


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                          SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


NAVARRO: Did you ever talk with them? Did they ever reach out to
you?

HUBBARD: On many issues, yes, but I don’t recall such a conversa-
tion about Iraq.

NAVARRO: Is it fair to say that the economy and economic issues
weren’t front and center in any decision making on the war?

HUBBARD: I can’t really say what was on the president’s mind.

NAVARRO: Now, there was a funny, tragic kind of incident. Larry
Lindsey, who was the assistant to President Bush on economic policy
while you were in the office, and a colleague of ours when we were
back at Harvard as PhD students, has gotten the rather unique dis-
tinction of getting fired for giving an estimate for the cost of the war
that was considered far too high by the administration when that esti-
mate turned out to be far too low. At the time, was Mr. Lindsey mak-
ing some kind of principled stand or statement about the war with that
estimate? Or did he simply underestimate the firestorm that would
erupt over those estimates?

HUBBARD: Larry Lindsey is an extremely talented economist, and
he also was an incredibly devoted public servant to President Bush.
He made a calculation about the effects of the war, but I’m not sure
putting it out was the wisest thing to do. But I don’t think that’s why
Larry Lindsey left the White House.

NAVARRO: Okay, this is a little bit delicate, but you’ve been criticized
for this, and I’ll give you the opportunity to address it. On October 4,
2002, you were quoted saying that “The cost of any intervention
would be very small.” What did you base that assessment on? And was
that one of those circumstances where you wish you could have those
words back?


                                  280

    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


HUBBARD: Well, I don’t know the context of the question. If the
question was about the macroeconomic cost to the U.S. economy,
those costs are probably relatively modest, and the question is, rela-
tive to what? The president’s argument was that Saddam Hussein was
a clear and present danger. That baseline was horrific. So I don’t know
if you can assess whether an answer is right or wrong without know-
ing what you believe about that baseline.

NAVARRO: Did it occur to you, at the time, that the data the presi-
dent was getting about alleged WMDs [weapons of mass destruction]
in Iraq would turn out to be dead wrong?

HUBBARD: I’m not an expert there, so I wouldn’t have an educated
view one way or another.

NAVARRO: If you’re getting evidence from the CIA that’s saying one
thing, it’s pretty reasonable to assume that it’s accurate. But I was
wondering if there was anybody who questioned that, including your-
self. But you’re saying no?

HUBBARD: I had no basis to question the judgment. There may have
been others who questioned the information.

NAVARRO: Let’s talk a little bit about Paul O’Neill and Alan
Greenspan. Historically, there’s always been tension between the
Secretary of the Treasury and the Chairman of the Council of
Economic Advisers, and one of the most epic battles occurred
between Don Regan and one of our mentors, Marty Feldstein, during
the Reagan administration. Can you just describe your relationship
with Treasury Secretary Paul O’Neill? And were you able to coordi-
nate with the Treasury? Or was O’Neill, as the press often described
him, a maverick or a lone wolf?




                                  281


                                             Download from www.wowebook.com
                         SEEDS OF DESTRUCTION


HUBBARD: Paul O’Neill returned to government a very successful
businessperson. He also brought a great deal of experience and suc-
cess in government. He had a bit of a disadvantage of not being with
the president in his campaign and not really having worked to design
the new policies. He may have lacked ownership of the ideas. Of
course, the president is in charge. Whether you’re Paul O’Neill or
Glenn Hubbard, you are just staff to the president himself. That may
have been a challenge for a business leader. But our relationship was
very cordial. The economic team worked together very well. We didn’t
always agree, but we still worked together well.

NAVARRO: And you met with O’Neill fairly frequently?

HUBBARD: Very often.

NAVARRO: And how about Fed Chairman Alan Greenspan? Can you
talk about how you worked with him, particularly after 9/11, and
whether he was aloof or cooperative? What was his general approach
to the White House?

HUBBARD: Greenspan’s success in understanding the productivity
expansion of the 1990s is noteworthy. And I would describe Alan
Greenspan as very cooperative, and in many circumstances a very
good mentor for me. He encouraged me to put together a group of
business leaders to talk with in real time about economic develop-
ments, particularly after 9/11. It was very useful for me to talk to a
group of CEOs and business executives regularly on where things
stood in the economy. Greenspan’s weakness, if you will, was he didn’t
always use economic models to evaluate data. Now, he would say that’s
his strength. But sometimes models are a very useful way to filter the
data. But I do think that he was an extraordinary public servant in
many respects, although I didn’t always agree with him on Federal
Reserve policy.


                                 282

NAVARRO: That’s a very interesting point about the model issue,
because when people talk about Greenspan in the press, they always
talk about how he consumed massive amounts of data. Are you saying
that he looked a lot at the data, but rather than have a formal model
or perspective, it was more of an ad hoc interpretation?

HUBBARD: The term “ad hoc” may sound pejorative. I think he was
somebody who had his own way of looking at large amounts of infor-
mation and filtering. The advantage of economic models is they give
you a lens to look at data and say, “If this, then that.” An ad hoc
approach can’t do that for you. Now, he would say, perhaps, the limi-
tation of those models is that they require critical assumptions. It’s not
that one is right or the other is wrong.

NAVARRO: According to the memoir that Treasury Secretary Paul
O’Neill wrote about his time with the Bush administration, he claimed
that both he and Alan Greenspan agreed that the proper policy action
was to use the Clinton budget surpluses to put Medicare and Social
Security on more solid footings and use them for tax cuts. Now, when
we talked about this earlier, you mentioned how you believed that any
surplus would be spent, rather than used for other things. Were those
views about using the budget surplus for Social Security and Medicare
made known to you by O’Neill at the time? And how would you
respond to the policy claim that that money should have gone to
Social Security and Medicare rather than tax cuts?

HUBBARD: I don’t ever recall Paul O’Neill sharing that observation
at the time. I will say that early on in the Bush campaign for president,
while he was still governor, Social Security reform was an issue that
came up in any discussion of budget surpluses. However, it was
Governor Bush’s conclusion that, politically, using the surplus for
Social Security was not likely to happen. Instead, the money would
simply be spent while you were wrangling. Therefore, it’s better to use

it for tax cuts. Ultimately, that’s really a political judgment. However,
I do share Secretary O’Neill’s and Chairman Greenspan’s view that we

NAVARRO: Let’s turn now, just briefly, to the housing bubble issue.
While you were Chairman of the Council of Economic Advisers, was
there any indication at the time that the easy-money policies of the
Federal Reserve would begin to fuel a housing bubble? Or was that
too soon in the process?

HUBBARD: Well, I think it was both too soon and on point. It was
too soon in the sense that I left in the spring of 2003, which was really
before the party really got going in housing. But there was a discus-
sion, even during the last few months I was there, and certainly imme-
diately thereafter, that the Fed’s actions would help stabilize the
economy by raising house prices. But that certainly had great risks to
it. I think the Fed’s view was that these risks would be so spread out
that no one would lose a lot if the bubble ever popped. And, of course,
that turned out not to be the case.

NAVARRO: So you believe that, even early on, there was at least some
recognition of the risks of a housing bubble associated with the very
low interest rates?

HUBBARD: Definitely. Remember that in the winter and spring of
2003, President Bush proposed and then got enacted a very signifi-
cant tax cut. The economy was recovering, and the idea that we
needed to continue the ultra-heated monetary policy was not one
shared by most of us.

NAVARRO: Why did you leave the White House?

HUBBARD: Well, for me, it was actually pretty simple. If you stay
more than two years, you really have to stay four, and as an academic
I really didn’t want to do that.
                                  284

    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


NAVARRO: Could you explain that? If you stay more than two years
you have to stay four?

HUBBARD: Well, the concern was really twofold in my case. One,
the president was going to run for reelection. And so, he deserved the
horses in place at the time of his reelection. If I had stayed, I’d need
to commit to stay through the election, so in effect, that was four
years. And it’s very hard on my family. My wife and children stayed in
New York and didn’t move to Washington.

NAVARRO: So you were commuting back and forth?

HUBBARD: I was commuting back and forth roughly every other
weekend.

NAVARRO: And you were on leave from Columbia at the time as a
professor?

HUBBARD: Right, on leave from Columbia.

NAVARRO: And so when you went back, you went back as Dean?

HUBBARD: No, I went back as a professor, and then about a year
later became Dean.

NAVARRO: On that note, and on a lighter note, one of the best videos
ever uploaded to YouTube is a parody by some of your Columbia
MBA students of your reaction to Ben Bernanke being appointed to
the Fed when you had been under strong consideration. Were you
aware that video was being made? And when you saw it, what was
your own reaction?

HUBBARD: No, I wasn’t aware of it. At the end of every semester,
our students have a Skit Night, and part of it is making fun of whoever
is Dean. I didn’t know they would do the video, but it was absolutely
hilarious. And I thought that night, this is interesting, but it probably

won’t go anywhere, and then the very next day I started getting calls

NAVARRO: Were you disappointed when Bernanke got appointed?
Were you expecting that? What went through your mind on that
whole thing?

HUBBARD: Well, two things. I think Bernanke was a very good
Federal Reserve chairman for the time. I don’t always agree with him,
but I think he has exactly the right temperament. And for a variety of
reasons, I knew I was not going to be that person.

NAVARRO: Do you think that your time at the White House and the
kind of circumstances in which you were in where all sorts of external
things happened to drag the economy down hurt your chances?

HUBBARD: No. I think it was probably a personal situation on my
part, point one. And point two, Ben was there in the White House at
the time and highly visible, and I think he was very, very qualified.

NAVARRO: Let’s move now to a big-picture question—what you
believe should be the proper role of the Council of Economic
Advisers. And there are really two broad views. The first one, let’s call
it the judicial or advisory view, is that the council should simply pro-
vide third-party advice on policies being considered by the White
House or Congress without any advocacy. The other, more activist
view is that the council should, and these are in your own words, play
“a very strong participatory role in developing economic policy.” With
the advantage of hindsight now, do you have any thoughts about what
the role of the chairman should be? Advisory or activist?

HUBBARD: Well, I think there are really two roles for any economic
advisor in the White House or, for that matter, the Treasury


                                  286


                                            Download from www.wowebook.com
    AN INTERVIEW WITH GLENN HUBBARD ABOUT HIS TIME IN THE WHITE HOUSE


Department. The first is that a lot of your time should be spent on try-
ing to beat down really bad ideas that could come from a number of
sources. But there are occasions where I think you can use your own
background knowledge to help advance policies proactively. I did that
unashamedly, and I still think that’s a good idea.

NAVARRO: And you think that’s what the chairman should do?

HUBBARD: I think that’s definitely what I intended to do, and did
do. It’s not to say that a chairman with a different view is wrong.

NAVARRO: Does it ever put you in an uncomfortable position, as an
academic, that you have to be more of an advocate than maybe the
data would thoroughly justify?

HUBBARD: Well, I never put myself in that position. I never justified
President Bush’s steel tariffs. I told him I’d make no effort to help him
there. When I spoke on policy, it was on issues that I actually believed
in. So, I never felt I had to make that compromise.

NAVARRO: Finally, in researching this interview, one of my favorite
quotes from the press about you was this: “Mr. Hubbard also had the
distinction of being one of Mr. Bush’s few representatives on eco-
nomic issues who did not get into trouble with embarrassing gaffes.”
Any concluding thoughts on that quote and your time in the White
House?

HUBBARD: When you have a job that’s in an official capacity, as
opposed to representing yourself, I always reminded myself that when
I spoke to an audience, they weren’t there to hear me. They were
there to hear me channel him [President Bush]. And so I think if you
remind yourself of that, you’re unlikely to make mistakes.
